Settlement

NadaTrade implements a full post-trade lifecycle: position tracking, realized P&L computation, settlement obligations, and mark-to-market valuation. This guide covers the mechanics of how your positions, cash, and settlement obligations are managed after each trade.

1. Position Tracking

Each participant holds one Position per instrument. A position is identified by (participant_id, instrument_id) and stored in the Ledger. A position has no explicit side field—the sign of qty determines the direction:

  • Positive qty → long position
  • Negative qty → short position
FieldTypeDescription
qtyDecimalCurrent position size. Positive = long, negative = short.
avg_costDecimalVolume-weighted average entry price.
realized_pnlDecimalCumulative realized profit/loss from closed portions of the position.
unrealized_pnlDecimalCurrent mark-to-market P&L (see §9).

Positions are lazily initialized to qty=0, avg_cost=0, realized_pnl=0, unrealized_pnl=0 on the first trade for that instrument.

2. apply_fill — Position Update Logic

Every TradePrinted event triggers an apply_fill call on both the buyer’s and the seller’s positions. A fill carries:

  • fill_qty: positive for buys, negative for sells (the sign matters)
  • fill_price: the execution price

The function handles three cases:

2a. Extend

When the fill is on the same side as the existing position (both qty values have the same sign), the position is extended:

new_qty = old_qty + fill_qty
avg_cost = (|old_qty| × old_avg + |fill_qty| × fill_price) / (|old_qty| + |fill_qty|)

Example. Holding +500 PETR4 at avg 38.50. Buy another +300 at 39.00:

avg_cost = (500 × 38.50 + 300 × 39.00) / (500 + 300) = 38.6875
new_qty  = +800
realized_pnl = 0 (no portion closed)

2b. Reduce

When the fill is on the opposite side (signs differ), a portion of the position is closed. Realized P&L is calculated on the closed portion only:

reduced = min(|old_qty|, |fill_qty|)
realized = reduced × (fill_price − avg_cost) × position_sign

The position sign (old_sign) is +1 for longs, −1 for shorts. This yields:

  • Closing longs (sell against long): reduced × (fill_price − avg_cost) — profit when fill_price > avg_cost
  • Closing shorts (buy back against short): reduced × (avg_cost − fill_price) — profit when fill_price < avg_cost

The remaining qty keeps the original avg_cost.

Example. Holding +500 PETR4 at 38.50. Sell 300 at 39.00:

reduced = min(500, 300) = 300
realized = 300 × (39.00 − 38.50) × (+1) = 150.00
new_qty  = +200
avg_cost = 38.50 (unchanged)

Example. Short −500 PETR4 at 40.00. Buy back 300 at 39.00:

reduced = min(500, 300) = 300
realized = 300 × (39.00 − 40.00) × (−1) = 300.00
new_qty  = −200
avg_cost = 40.00 (unchanged)

2c. Flip

When the fill qty exceeds the position in the opposite direction, the position flips:

  1. Close the entire original position → realize full P&L on the original side
  2. Open a new opposite-side position at the fill price
realized = |old_qty| × (fill_price − old_avg) × old_sign
avg_cost = fill_price

Example. Holding +500 PETR4 at 38.50. Sell 800 at 39.00:

realized = 500 × (39.00 − 38.50) × (+1) = 250.00
avg_cost = 39.00
new_qty  = −300 (now short)

Example. Short −500 PETR4 at 40.00. Buy 700 at 39.00:

realized = 500 × (39.00 − 40.00) × (−1) = 500.00
avg_cost = 39.00
new_qty  = +200 (now long)

3. The Ledger

The Ledger struct is the per-participant balance sheet:

FieldTypeDescription
participantParticipantIdOwner of this ledger.
cashHashMap<Currency, Decimal>Cash balances per currency.
positionsHashMap<InstrumentId, Position>One position per instrument traded.
pending_obligationsVec<SettlementObligation>Unsettled T+N obligations (see §6–8).
realized_pnlDecimalRunning total of all realized P&L across positions.

Cash tracking

On each trade, cash is adjusted immediately:

  • Buyer: cash − (qty × price) in the instrument’s currency
  • Seller: cash + (qty × price) in the instrument’s currency

Cash is tracked per currency (e.g., "BRL", "USD"). The currency comes from the instrument’s InstrumentConfig.settlement.currency field.

Ledger lifecycle

  • A ledger is created lazily on the first trade for a participant.
  • Positions are created lazily per instrument.
  • Settlement can later adjust cash (see §8).

4. Realized P&L

Realized P&L is credited to the realized_pnl field on the Ledger each time apply_fill closes any qty. The formula depends on direction:

DirectionActionFormula
LongSell (close long)qty × (sell_price − avg_cost)
ShortBuy (close short)qty × (avg_cost − buy_price)

Example — Long P&L. Bought 500 at 38.50. Sold 200 at 39.00:

P&L = 200 × (39.00 − 38.50) = +100.00

Example — Short P&L. Sold short 300 at 42.00. Bought back 150 at 40.00:

P&L = 150 × (42.00 − 40.00) = +300.00

Realized P&L is cumulative and never resets. It is not the same as mark-to-market (unrealized) P&L. Only closed portions generate realized P&L.

5. Settlement Cycle (T+N)

Each instrument defines a settlement cycle in its configuration under settlement.cycle:

CycleDescriptionSettlement date
T0Same-day settlementTrade date
T1Next business dayTrade date + 1 day
T2Two business days (default)Trade date + 2 days
T3Three business daysTrade date + 3 days
TN(int)Custom N daysTrade date + N days

Unknown cycle values default to T2.

The settlement cycle is configured per instrument:

[[instruments]]
name = "PETR4"
# ...
[instruments.settlement]
cycle = "T2"
currency = "BRL"

6. Settlement Date Calculation

The settlement date is calculated as trade_date + N calendar days, where N is determined by the instrument’s settlement cycle (§5).

For session-based instruments with trading_days, the system uses InstrumentConfig.trading_days (e.g., ["Mon", "Tue", "Wed", "Thu", "Fri"]) to skip weekends and holidays when needed by higher-level scheduling logic. The current implementation computes the settlement date as a simple calendar offset from the trade’s sim_time.

This means a trade on Friday with T+2 would settle on Sunday (calendar date). The sweep function then checks if the current simulation date equals or exceeds this date.

7. Settlement Obligations

When create_obligations() is called for each TradePrinted event, it creates one SettlementObligation per side of the trade:

FieldDescription
trade_idThe UUID of the trade.
participantWhich participant owes or is owed.
instrumentThe instrument traded.
qtyQuantity traded.
priceExecution price.
sideSide::Buy or Side::Sell.
cashNotional amount (qty × price).
currencySettlement currency (from instrument config).
settle_dateThe date when this obligation becomes due.
settledfalse initially; set to true after sweep.

Buyer’s obligation: cash is positive (the amount owed to the counterparty). On settlement, this amount is debited from the buyer’s cash balance.

Seller’s obligation: cash is also positive (the amount the seller receives). On settlement, this amount is credited to the seller’s cash balance.

Obligations are appended to the participant’s pending_obligations list in their ledger.

Synthetic trades

Trades with synthetic = true (e.g., from simulation or mark-to-market adjustments) are skipped in settlement — they generate no obligations. Only real trades originating from the matching engine create obligations.

8. Settlement Sweep

sweep_settlements() runs periodically. In the current implementation, it is triggered at end-of-day (when the session transitions to Closed).

Sweep logic

For each participant’s pending obligations:

  1. If settle_date ≤ today and settled == false:
    • Buyer side: debit cash from the participant’s cash balance.
    • Seller side: credit cash to the participant’s cash balance.
    • Mark the obligation as settled = true.
    • Emit an ObligationSettled event.
  2. If settle_date > today, leave the obligation in the pending list.

Future-dated obligations are never settled early. Once settled, obligations are retained in the pending list with settled = true (they are not removed).

Settlement events

Each settled obligation emits an ExchangeEvent::ObligationSettled:

{
  "participant": "BOT001",
  "trade_id": "f47ac10b-...",
  "instrument": "PETR4",
  "qty": "100.0",
  "cash": "3850.00",
  "currency": "BRL",
  "sim_time": ...
}

Worked example

  • BOT001 buys 100 PETR4 @ 38.50 from BOT002 on Monday.
  • Settlement cycle: T2, so settle_date = Wednesday.
  • On Monday and Tuesday the sweep runs but skips this obligation (settle_date is in the future).
  • On Wednesday, the sweep fires:
    • BOT001 cash: −3,850.00 BRL
    • BOT002 cash: +3,850.00 BRL
    • Obligation marked settled.

Mixed obligations

A participant can have obligations with different settlement dates. The sweep processes only those that have matured:

pending_obligations = [
  { settle_date: 2026-06-09, settled: true  },  // already swept earlier
  { settle_date: 2026-06-12, settled: false },  // sweeps today
  { settle_date: 2026-06-15, settled: false },  // stays pending
]

9. Mark-to-Market (Unrealized P&L)

mark_to_market() computes the unrealized P&L on open positions based on the most recent trade price:

unrealized_pnl = current_qty × (last_price − avg_cost)

This is called on every TradePrinted event, using the trade price as the mark. For example:

  • Position: long 1,000 PETR4 @ avg 38.50

  • Last trade: 40.00

  • Unrealized P&L = 1000 × (40.00 − 38.50) = +1,500.00 (BRL, since PETR4 is BRL-denominated)

  • Position: short 500 PETR4 @ avg 42.00

  • Last trade: 40.00

  • Unrealized P&L = −500 × (40.00 − 42.00) = +1,000.00

Key properties

  • Informational only. Mark-to-market does not affect cash balances, realized P&L, or settlement.
  • Per-instrument. Only positions in the instrument whose trade just occurred are re-marked. Other instruments retain their last mark.
  • Flat positions ignored. If qty == 0, unrealized P&L is left unchanged.
  • Used by the TUI Dashboard (Ledger tab), risk monitoring, and external reporting.

10. Synthetic Trades

A TradePrinted event carries a synthetic boolean field:

  • synthetic: false — real trade from the matching engine. Positions are updated, obligations are created, settlement applies.
  • synthetic: true — generated trade (e.g., from simulation scenarios, replay data, or system adjustments). Position tracking still applies, but no settlement obligations are created. Realized P&L is still computed for the ledger.

This allows synthetic data feeds to exercise position tracking without polluting the settlement pipeline.

11. Continuous Instruments (24×7)

Instruments with session.type = "continuous_24x7" never enter a Closed session state, so the EOD sweep trigger does not fire. For these instruments:

  • Settlement obligations are still created with appropriate settle_date values (T+0, T+2, etc.).
  • The sweep function processes obligations whenever it is called. Integration code should invoke sweep_settlements() on a periodic timer (e.g., every simulated midnight) for continuous instruments.
  • No trading-day gating applies — every calendar day is a valid settlement date.

The trading_days configuration (e.g., ["Mon", "Tue", "Wed", "Thu", "Fri"]) does not constrain settlement for continuous instruments.

12. Currency

Every instrument declares a settlement currency:

[instruments.settlement]
cycle = "T2"
currency = "BRL"

All cash tracking and settlement amounts use this currency. A single participant’s ledger can hold balances in multiple currencies (e.g., BRL from PETR4 trades, USD from ADR trades). Each currency bucket is tracked independently in ledger.cash: HashMap<Currency, Decimal>.

Currency flow summary

StepWhere the currency field is used
Trade executionInstrumentConfig.settlement.currency
Cash debit/creditsettle_trade() adjusts ledger.cash[currency]
Obligation recordSettlementObligation.currency
Settlement sweepSweep moves cash using the obligation’s currency field
MtM displayP&L is expressed in the instrument’s currency

Full Lifecycle Example

Consider BOT001 trading PETR4 (currency = BRL, T+2 settlement):

DayEventPositionCash (BRL)Pending Obligations
Mon 09:30Buy 1,000 @ 38.50+1,000 @ 38.50−38,5001 (settles Wed)
Mon 10:00Sell 300 @ 39.00+700 @ 38.50−26,8001 (qty 1,000)
Mon 10:01Realized P&L: 300 × (39.00 − 38.50) = +150.00
Wed 00:01Sweep runs (settle_date ≤ today)+700 @ 38.50−26,8001 (settled)

After settlement, BOT001’s cash reflects the full consideration for the trade. The realized P&L of 150.00 is embedded in the position tracking but does not change the cash flow (the seller receives the full 38,500 regardless of BOT001’s average cost).

The unrealized P&L on the remaining 700 shares will fluctuate with each subsequent trade price update via mark_to_market, visible in the TUI Dashboard Ledger tab.

Configuration Reference

KeyTypeDefaultDescription
instruments[].settlement.cycle"T0", "T1", "T2", "T3", or "TN(int)"—Settlement cycle duration.
instruments[].settlement.currencystring—ISO currency code for cash and obligations.
instruments[].session.trading_daysstring[]["Mon","Tue","Wed","Thu","Fri"]Valid trading days (for business-day logic).
instruments[].session.type"session_based" or "continuous_24x7"—Session model; affects sweep timing.

See Also

  • Matching Engine — how fills are generated
  • Risk Pipeline — position limits use the ledger tracked here
  • Persistence — how positions survive restarts
  • Simulation — how settlement sweep is triggered in different clock modes

Persistence

NadaTrade uses an event sourcing persistence model backed by PostgreSQL. There is no mutable “current state” database. All system state is reconstructed from two tables: an append-only event log and periodic CLOB snapshots. This foundation enables full auditability and deterministic replay.

Tables

NadaTrade creates five tables on first start (via sqlx::migrate!):

TablePurpose
eventsAppend-only event log, partitioned monthly on occurred_at
snapshotsPeriodic full serializations of engine state per instrument
book_snapshotsLegacy table — superseded by snapshots
ledger_snapshotsParticipant position snapshots
persist_metaKey-value metadata (last persisted sequence, etc.)

Events table

The events table is the authoritative record of everything that happened in the system. It is append-only — rows are never updated or deleted.

CREATE TABLE events (
    sequence       BIGINT       NOT NULL,
    occurred_at    TIMESTAMPTZ  NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
    sim_time       TIMESTAMPTZ  NOT NULL,
    instrument_id  TEXT,
    participant_id TEXT,
    event_type     TEXT         NOT NULL,
    payload        JSONB        NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (occurred_at, sequence)
) PARTITION BY RANGE (occurred_at);

Columns:

  • sequence — globally unique, monotonically increasing event number. Assigned by the binary at fan-out time.
  • occurred_at — wall-clock time the event was persisted (PostgreSQL server clock).
  • sim_time — simulation time extracted from the event. This is the authoritative business time regardless of clock mode (RealTime, Accelerated, or Replay).
  • instrument_id — the instrument this event pertains to, or NULL for system-wide events (SystemMessage, ClockSpeedChanged, etc.).
  • participant_id — the participant who originated this event, or NULL for book/session/system events.
  • event_type — snake_case variant name: order_accepted, order_rejected, order_filled, order_partially_filled, order_cancelled, order_expired, order_replaced, cancel_rejected, trade_printed, book_updated, session_state_changed, auction_uncrossed, reference_price_changed, obligation_settled, scenario_applied, clock_speed_changed, replay_started, replay_stopped, system_message.
  • payload — the full serialized ExchangeEvent as JSONB.

Indexes:

CREATE INDEX idx_events_seq         ON events (sequence);
CREATE INDEX idx_events_instr_seq   ON events (instrument_id, sequence);
CREATE INDEX idx_events_type        ON events (event_type, sequence);
CREATE INDEX idx_events_part_seq    ON events (participant_id, sequence) WHERE participant_id IS NOT NULL;

Partitioning

The events table uses monthly range partitioning on occurred_at. Child partitions are named events_yYYYYMM:

events
├── events_y202606  (2026-06-01 to 2026-07-01)
├── events_y202607  (2026-07-01 to 2026-08-01)
└── ...

Partitions are created lazily — on the first flush into a new month, ensure_current_partition() issues CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS events_yYYYYMM PARTITION OF events FOR VALUES FROM (...) . The current month is cached in last_partition_month so the partition check runs at most once per month, not on every flush.

Querying

The partition key (occurred_at) is in the primary key, so range scans on wall-clock time use partition pruning automatically. To query by simulation time or event type, combine with the corresponding indexes:

SELECT * FROM events
WHERE sim_time BETWEEN '2026-06-01' AND '2026-06-02'
ORDER BY sequence;

Snapshots table

Replaying every event since system start to recover current state would be prohibitively slow. The snapshots table stores periodic full serializations of engine state for each instrument:

CREATE TABLE snapshots (
    instrument_id   TEXT        NOT NULL,
    snapshot_at     TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL,
    sequence        BIGINT      NOT NULL,
    session_state   JSONB       NOT NULL,
    clob_state      JSONB       NOT NULL,
    stop_book_state JSONB       NOT NULL,
    expiries_state  JSONB       NOT NULL,
    state           JSONB       NOT NULL,
    PRIMARY KEY (instrument_id, snapshot_at)
);

Each row captures the complete engine state at a point in time:

  • session_state — the session state machine state (Closed, PreOpening, OpeningAuction, Trading, ClosingAuction, Closed, TradingHalt, ReopeningAuction).
  • clob_state — full order book: bids and asks as BTreeMap<Price, VecDeque<RestingOrder>>.
  • stop_book_state — stop order book (untriggered stop and stop-limit orders).
  • expiries_state — pending GTD (Good-Till-Date) expiry queue.
  • state — engine-level auxiliary state.
  • sequence — the global sequence number this snapshot corresponds to.

Index:

CREATE INDEX idx_snapshots_instr_seq ON snapshots (instrument_id, sequence);

Snapshot pruning

Each time a new snapshot is written for an instrument, NadaTrade prunes older entries for that instrument, retaining only the most recent 3 snapshots. This prevents unbounded growth in the snapshots table while ensuring enough history for diagnostic purposes.

EventWriter

The EventWriter is the core persistence actor. It lives behind an Arc<Mutex<EventWriter>> and is called from the main event fan-out loop.

Buffering and flush

Events are buffered in memory until one of two thresholds triggers a flush:

  1. Batch size — when buffer.len() >= batch_max, the writer flushes immediately after write().
  2. Wall-clock timer — a periodic flush runs at batch_max_ms interval, ensuring events are persisted even at low throughput.

Both thresholds are configurable. Whichever triggers first executes the flush.

During a flush, the writer:

  1. Checks if we’ve entered a new month — calls ensure_current_partition() to create the next monthly partition if needed.
  2. Takes the buffered events (leaving an empty buffer behind).
  3. Opens a PostgreSQL transaction.
  4. Inserts each event row with INSERT INTO events ....
  5. Updates persist_meta with key = 'last_sequence' → the highest-sequence event in this batch, using ON CONFLICT ... DO UPDATE.
  6. Commits the transaction.

All events in a batch are committed atomically. If any insert fails, the entire batch is rolled back and the error is returned.

Metrics

The writer exports Prometheus metrics:

MetricTypeDescription
nadatrade_persist_queue_depthGaugeNumber of events buffered waiting for flush
nadatrade_persist_batch_flush_msHistogramWall-clock duration of each flush transaction

Noop mode

When no database URL is configured, EventWriter::new_noop() is used. Events are still buffered and flush clears the buffer, but no database calls are made. This is useful for development, testing, or when persistence is intentionally disabled.

Snapshots

Auto-snapshot triggering

The main event loop monitors per-instrument event counters via EventWriter::should_snapshot(instrument, threshold). When the counter reaches snapshot_every_events (default: 10,000), the engine serializes its state via serialize_snapshot(global_seq) and the writer persists it to the snapshots table.

The counter is per-instrument — tracked in events_since_snapshot: HashMap<String, u64> and incremented on every event that has an instrument_id. After a snapshot is written, the counter is reset to zero via reset_snapshot_count().

The EngineSnapshot struct contains:

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
pub struct EngineSnapshot {
    pub sequence: u64,
    pub session_state: serde_json::Value,
    pub clob_state: serde_json::Value,
    pub stop_book_state: serde_json::Value,
    pub expiries_state: serde_json::Value,
    pub state: serde_json::Value,
}
}

Each component is a serde_json::Value — the engine serializes its internal BTreeMap-based state to JSON. On recovery, these are deserialized back into the engine’s native structures.

Recovery flow

On restart, the system reconstructs state through load_recovery_data():

  1. Read persist_meta — query SELECT value FROM persist_meta WHERE key = 'last_sequence' to find the last persisted sequence number. If 0, this is a fresh database — return empty state.

  2. Load newest snapshots — for each instrument, query the most recent snapshot (highest snapshot_at) using SELECT DISTINCT ON (instrument_id) ... ORDER BY instrument_id, snapshot_at DESC. Each snapshot includes the sequence number it was taken at.

  3. Load events since snapshot — find the minimum snapshot sequence across all instruments (min_seq). Query SELECT sequence, payload FROM events WHERE sequence > min_seq ORDER BY sequence ASC. If no snapshots exist, all events are loaded.

  4. Replay events — events are deserialized from JSONB and replayed through the matching engine in sequence order. The engine applies each event, rebuilding the CLOB, stop book, session state, and expiry queues. After replay, the engine is ready to accept new orders.

The recovery data is returned as RecoveryState:

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
pub struct RecoveryState {
    pub last_sequence: u64,
    pub instrument_states: HashMap<String, (u64, serde_json::Value)>,
    pub events: Vec<(u64, serde_json::Value)>,
}
}

Recovery with snapshots (fast path)

If snapshots exist:

  • Each engine is initialized from its snapshot state (deserialized JSON → native structures).
  • Only events after the snapshot sequence are replayed.
  • Typical recovery time: seconds, even with millions of historical events.

Recovery without snapshots (slow path)

If no snapshots exist (fresh install, or snapshots were deleted):

  • Engines start empty.
  • All events from the events table are replayed sequentially.
  • Recovery time scales linearly with event count. For systems with hundreds of millions of events, configure snapshot frequency appropriately.

Configuration

All persistence settings live under [persist] in the config file:

[database]
url = "postgres://user:pass@localhost/nadatrade"
max_connections = 8

[persist]
batch_max = 500
batch_max_ms = 50
snapshot_every_events = 10000
queue_warn_depth = 5000
KeyDefaultDescription
[database].urlNonePostgreSQL connection string. If absent, persistence runs in noop mode.
[database].max_connections8Connection pool size passed to sqlx::PgPool.
persist.batch_max500Maximum events buffered before an automatic flush.
persist.batch_max_ms50Maximum wall-clock milliseconds between periodic flushes.
persist.snapshot_every_events10000Per-instrument event count threshold for auto-snapshot.
persist.queue_warn_depth5000Queue depth at which a warning is logged (monitoring).

Tuning guide

  • High-throughput trading — increase batch_max (1000–2000) to reduce transaction overhead. Consider raising snapshot_every_events (50,000–100,000) to avoid snapshot writes during peak activity.
  • Low-latency — decrease batch_max_ms (10–25ms) for near-real-time durability at the cost of more frequent transactions.
  • Recovery time budget — tune snapshot_every_events so the worst-case replay count (events since last snapshot) fits your recovery SLA. At 10,000 events/second, snapshot_every_events = 10000 means ≤1 second of replay.
  • Storage — monthly partitions let you drop old data with DROP TABLE events_y202506 rather than DELETE (which vacuums). No application-level partition dropping is built in — manage retention externally or with cron.

Disabling persistence

Omit the [database] section entirely:

# No [database] = noop persistence (in-memory only)
[server]
# ...

All events will be published to subscribers and the TUI will function, but no data survives a restart.

See Also

  • Architecture — snapshot creation and recovery flow
  • Settlement — how settlement obligations are recreated on recovery
  • Testing Guide — testing persistence with Docker
  • Config Keys — [persist] and [database] configuration

Simulation

NadaTrade’s clock and latency layer controls how time advances, how network latency is modeled, and how test scenarios are injected. It is the backbone for testing, bot development, and production rehearsal.

Overview

The simulation subsystem lives in crates/nadatrade-simulation/ and provides four capabilities:

ComponentRole
SimClockSource of truth for all business-logic time
ClockDriverEmits periodic ClockTick commands to drive the event loop
LatencySimulatorAdds realistic per-participant command delays
ScenarioInjectorInjects 7 types of synthetic events into the system

All business code reads time from SimClock::now(). Direct calls to chrono::Utc::now(), tokio::time::sleep, or Instant::now in business logic are forbidden (only allowed in gateway heartbeats, metrics, DB batching, and the sim subsystem itself).

Clock Modes

NadaTrade supports three clock modes, selected via [simulation] config:

[simulation]
mode = "realtime"      # or "accelerated" or "replay"

Mode Comparison

PropertyRealTimeAcceleratedReplay
Time sourceSystem wall clockWall × factorNDJSON feed records
wall_per_sim_sec()1s1000 / factor msNone
Tick sourceClockDriver taskClockDriver taskFeed reader task
PausableNoYes (via AcceleratedClock::pause())Yes (via ReplayDrivenClock::set_time())
Use caseProduction test, live botBacktest, fast-forwardHistorical replay

RealTime Mode

1:1 mapping to wall clock. Every wall second equals one simulation second.

[simulation]
mode = "realtime"

The RealTimeClock returns Utc::now() and reports wall_per_sim_sec() == 1s. The ClockDriver sleeps exactly one wall second between each ClockTick. This mode is ideal for live bot development, FIX/WS integration testing, and observing session transitions in real time.

Accelerated Mode

Simulation time runs faster than wall clock by a configurable factor.

[simulation]
mode = "accelerated"
clock_speed = 60.0     # 1 wall second = 60 sim seconds (1 minute)

The AcceleratedClock tracks a wall Instant at construction, then multiplies elapsed wall milliseconds by clock_speed to compute simulation time:

sim_time = sim_start + (wall_elapsed_ms × clock_speed)

wall_per_sim_sec() returns 1000 / clock_speed ms, so the ClockDriver ticks every 1000/60 ≈ 17ms when clock_speed = 60. Higher factors mean faster simulation.

Pause / Resume

The AcceleratedClock can pause and resume. When paused, now() returns the time at the moment of pause. Useful for inspecting state mid-backtest.

# via OrderCommand (sent from TUI or API)
OrderCommand::Scenario { event: ScenarioEvent::BroadcastSystemMessage { ... } }

When speed changes, a ClockSpeedChanged exchange event is emitted with the new factor.

Replay Mode

Driven by an NDJSON feed file. Each record in the feed sets the simulation clock to its sim_time and then the system processes the next event.

[simulation]
mode = "replay"
replay_feed = "/data/feed.ndjson"
sim_start = "2024-01-02T09:30:00-05:00"

Feed Format

The NDJSON feed contains one JSON object per line. Two record types are supported:

Trade records:

{"sim_time":"2024-01-15T10:00:00.123Z","instrument":"PETR4","type":"trade","price":"38.42","qty":"1000","aggressor_side":"buy"}

Quote records:

{"sim_time":"2024-01-15T10:00:01Z","instrument":"PETR4","type":"quote","bid":"38.40","bid_qty":"5000","ask":"38.45","ask_qty":"3000"}

Fields:

FieldTradeQuoteDescription
sim_timeYesYesISO-8601 timestamp (sets the clock)
instrumentYesYesInstrument ID (must match registry)
typeYesYes"trade" or "quote"
priceYes—Trade price (string)
qtyYes—Trade quantity (string)
aggressor_sideYes—"buy" or "sell"
bid—YesBid price (string)
bid_qty—YesBid quantity (string)
ask—YesAsk price (string)
ask_qty—YesAsk quantity (string)

Replay Behavior

The MarketReplay::from_ndjson() parser reads all lines on startup. Records iterate in order via next_record(). Each record:

  1. Sets the ReplayDrivenClock time to record.sim_time
  2. Sends OrderCommand::ClockTick { sim_time } down the command channel
  3. The engine processes the tick (drives session state machine, triggers scheduled actions)

The feed reader inserts a 1ms sleep between records to allow the event loop to process. This is not pacing — it’s simply yielding to the async runtime.

replay_pacing (planned): when true, the feed reader waits for wall clock to align with record timestamps, creating realistic gaps. When false (current default), records are processed as fast as the 1ms yield allows.

sim_start overrides the feed’s initial timestamp. If omitted, the clock starts at the timestamp of the first record in the feed.

Replay Controls (via TUI)

When the TUI is enabled with [tui] enabled = true, the Clock tab provides replay controls:

ActionKeybindingEffect
PausepStops processing feed records (clock freezes)
ResumerContinues processing feed records
Jump to timejPrompts for ISO timestamp; sets clock directly
Speed adjust+/-Changes replay speed factor (planned)

The system emits ReplayStarted and ReplayStopped events when the feed begins and ends/exhausts.

Generating Feed Files

Use the replay-convert tool in tools/replay-convert/ to convert B3, NYSE, or Binance historical data to NDJSON:

cargo run -p replay-convert -- --input nyse_trades.csv --output feed.ndjson

ClockDriver

ClockDriver is the bridge between a SimClock and the command channel. It runs as an async task:

  1. Queries clock.wall_per_sim_sec() to get the sleep duration
  2. Sleeps for that duration
  3. Reads clock.now() for the current sim time
  4. Sends OrderCommand::ClockTick { sim_time } to the command channel
  5. Repeats
#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
// Simplified: ClockDriver::run()
loop {
    if let Some(wall_dur) = self.clock.wall_per_sim_sec() {
        tokio::time::sleep(wall_dur).await;
    }
    let now = self.clock.now();
    cmd_tx.send(OrderCommand::ClockTick { sim_time: now }).await?;
}
}

In RealTime mode, this fires every 1 wall second. In Accelerated mode, it fires every 1000/clock_speed ms. In Replay mode, the ClockDriver is not used — the feed reader task sends ticks directly.

Latency Simulation

The LatencySimulator sits between gateways and the matching engine, inserting realistic per-participant delays on incoming orders.

Channel Pipeline

Gateway → LatencySimulator → PreTrade → MatchingEngine

When latency_seed is set in config, the run_latency_middleware task intercepts every OrderCommand from the gateway channel. It looks up the participant’s latency profile, computes a delay, and sleeps before forwarding the command.

If latency_seed is absent, a pass-through task forwards commands with zero delay.

Latency Profiles

Five built-in profiles are available, configured per participant in their TOML file:

ProfileMean DelayStd DevSamplingDescription
"none"0 µs—FixedNo delay (default)
{ fixed = { micros = N } }N µs0FixedExact fixed delay
"co_location"50 µs10 µsNormalExchange colocation
"remote"5,000 µs1,500 µsNormalSatellite/remote
{ normal = { mean_us = M, stddev_us = S } }M µsS µsNormalCustom profile

Box-Muller Sampling

Profiles using normal distribution use the Box-Muller transform for realistic delay variation:

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
let z = (-2.0 * u.ln()).sqrt() * (2.0 * PI * v).cos();
mean + stddev * z
}

Results are clamped to zero (no negative delays). The underlying RNG is a seeded StdRng, so latency_seed = 42 produces the same delay sequence every run.

Configuring Latency per Participant

In a participant TOML file:

[participants.super_bot]
comp_id = "SUPER_BOT"
display_name = "Super Trading Bot"
initial_cash = { USD = "1000000.00" }

# Latency profile examples (choose one):
latency_profile = "none"                    # No delay
latency_profile = "co_location"            # ~50µs normal delay
latency_profile = "remote"                 # ~5000µs normal delay
latency_profile = { fixed = { micros = 250 } }  # Exactly 250µs
latency_profile = { normal = { mean_us = 500, stddev_us = 100 } }  # Custom

The default profile is "none", simulating a participant with zero network latency.

Market Data Staggering

In addition to command latency, each participant can experience staggered market data arrival via the md_extra_delay_us field:

[participants.slow_broker]
comp_id = "SLOW_BROKER"
latency_profile = "co_location"
md_extra_delay_us = 750   # Additional 750µs before MD messages arrive

How It Works

  1. The LatencySimulator::md_delay_for() method computes the per-participant MD delay
  2. md_extra_delay_us always takes priority when set — it overrides any derived value from the latency profile
  3. When md_extra_delay_us is absent, the delay is derived from the profile:
    • None → 0 µs
    • CoLocation → 50 µs
    • Remote → 5,000 µs
    • Fixed { micros } → that exact value
    • Normal { mean_us, .. } → the mean value
  4. The delay is applied in both the FIX acceptor (before dispatching market data messages to a FIX session) and the WS server (before sending market data frames to a WebSocket client)

This creates realistic staggered arrival of market data updates across participants, even when the market data itself is generated simultaneously by the matching engine.

Scenario Injection

Seven scenario types can be injected at runtime via OrderCommand::Scenario, enabling testing of market events, regulatory actions, and infrastructure failures without disrupting live data.

Scenario Types

#VariantEffect
1haltPlaces an instrument into TradingHalt. Optional duration_secs for auto-resume; without it, manual resume is required.
2resumeForces an instrument back to ContinuousTrading from any halted state.
3set_reference_priceOverrides the reference price used by circuit breakers and auction uncrossing.
4trigger_circuit_breakerImmediately triggers a circuit breaker halt for the instrument.
5expire_gtcExpires all GTC orders for the instrument at the current sim time.
6delist_instrumentRemoves an instrument from trading entirely, with a reason string.
7broadcastSends a BroadcastSystemMessage event with arbitrary text.

ScenarioEvent Enum (from nadatrade-core)

#![allow(unused)]
fn main() {
pub enum ScenarioEvent {
    HaltInstrument {
        duration_secs: Option<u64>,
        reason: HaltReason,
    },
    ResumeInstrument,
    SetReferencePrice {
        price: Decimal,
    },
    TriggerCircuitBreaker,
    ExpireAllGtc,
    DelistInstrument {
        instrument: String,
        reason: String,
    },
    BroadcastSystemMessage {
        text: String,
    },
}
}

Via TUI Keybindings

When the TUI is active, scenarios can be injected with keyboard shortcuts:

KeyScenario
hHalt instrument
rResume instrument
dDelist instrument
sTrigger circuit breaker

Via WebSocket / API

Scenarios travel as OrderCommand::Scenario through the same command channel as orders. The WS server routes them:

{
  "type": "scenario",
  "event": {
    "halt_instrument": {
      "duration_secs": 300,
      "reason": "manual_halt"
    }
  },
  "instrument": "PETR4"
}

When a scenario is processed by the engine, a ScenarioApplied exchange event is emitted, containing the original ScenarioEvent wrapped for observability.

Configuration Reference

Full [simulation] section schema:

[simulation]
mode = "replay"                       # "realtime" | "accelerated" | "replay"
clock_speed = 60.0                    # float; accelerator factor (default: 1.0)
replay_feed = "/data/feed.ndjson"     # path to NDJSON replay file (replay mode only)
sim_start = "2024-01-02T09:30:00Z"    # override replay start time (ISO-8601)
latency_seed = 42                     # u64 seed for reproducible latency RNG

Key Details

KeyTypeDefaultNotes
modestring"" → realtimeCase-insensitive: "realtime", "accelerated", "replay"
clock_speedf641.0Used only in accelerated mode. Must be > 0.
replay_feedpathNoneRequired for replay mode. File is read entirely at startup.
sim_startISO-8601First feed record timeOverrides initial clock in replay mode. Also used as anchor for AcceleratedClock.
latency_seedu64None (disabled)If set, enables the latency middleware with deterministic RNG.

Common Configurations

Production testing (realtime):

[simulation]
mode = "realtime"
clock_speed = 1.0

Backtesting at 60× speed:

[simulation]
mode = "accelerated"
clock_speed = 60.0

Historical replay with reproducible latency:

[simulation]
mode = "replay"
replay_feed = "/data/2024-01-15_feed.ndjson"
sim_start = "2024-01-15T09:00:00-03:00"
latency_seed = 42

Zero-latency fast replay:

[simulation]
mode = "replay"
replay_feed = "/data/feed.ndjson"
# latency_seed omitted → no latency middleware → direct pass-through

Determinism & Reproducibility

  • Same seed, same results. With latency_seed = N, the Box-Muller RNG produces identical delay sequences across runs. Feed records always iterate in order.
  • No RNG in matching core. The matching engine is fully deterministic. Latency simulation is the only source of stochasticity.
  • BTreeMap for ordering. Hot-path collections use BTreeMap, not HashMap, guaranteeing deterministic iteration order.
  • Replay identical. Running the same feed file twice with the same latency_seed produces identical trade outcomes — critical for backtest validation and regression testing.

See Also

  • Sessions — how ClockTick drives session transitions
  • Matching Engine — how stop expiry and circuit breaker use sim_time
  • Config Keys — [simulation] configuration reference
  • Examples — full_session example demonstrates session+clock integration

TUI Dashboard

The NadaTrade terminal UI (built on [ratatui] + [crossterm]) provides a real-time dashboard into the exchange. It displays instrument state, order books, event logs, clock controls, and ledger data — all updated live from the same broadcast channel the rest of the system consumes.

Launch

Embedded mode — Set [tui] enabled = true in your config to start the TUI inside the main binary process:

[tui]
enabled = true

cargo run -p nadatrade-bin -- --config config/nadatrade.toml

When enabled is set, main.rs spawns the TUI on a dedicated std::thread before entering the main event loop. It clones the gateway_cmd_tx sender and subscribes to the event_tx broadcast channel, so every SequencedEvent flowing through the system is mirrored to the TUI.

Standalone mode — Run the TUI crate directly. It connects to the broadcast channel via the same process (no separate socket — the TUI runs in-process):

cargo run -p nadatrade-admin

The TUI runs until you press q or Esc. Exiting the TUI does not stop the exchange — it only detaches the visual dashboard. You can restart it at any time.

Keybindings

KeyAction
Tab / →Next tab
Shift+Tab / ←Previous tab
↑ / PageUpScroll event log up (older events)
↓ / PageDownScroll event log down (newer events)
HomeJump to top of event log
EndJump to bottom of event log
q / EscQuit (works in all tabs, including Admin)

Tab selection wraps around — pressing Tab on the last tab returns you to the first.

Tab 1 — Instruments

A sortable table of every instrument the exchange knows about, one row per instrument. Rows are sorted alphabetically by symbol.

┌─ NadaTrade v0.1.4 ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

│ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │

│                                                                              │

ColumnContent
SymbolInstrument ID (e.g. PETR4, VALE3)

AskBest ask: price and total quantity at that level

Session state colors:

ColorState(s)
GreenTrading
YellowOpeningAuction, ReopeningAuction, ClosingAuction, PreOpen
RedTradingHalt(...) — reason shown in parentheses

State transitions are driven by SessionStateChanged events. The table initializes empty and populates as instruments become active.

Tab 2 — Order Book

Shows the top 5 price levels for each instrument. Uses a vertical layout — instruments stack top to bottom, each showing bids on the left (green) and asks on the right (red).

┌─ NadaTrade v0.1.4 ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

│    Price         Qty            │   Price         Qty                        │

Bids (green, left side): highest price first — best bid at the top.
Asks (red, right side): lowest price first — best ask at the top.

Levels display price (right-aligned, 12-width) and quantity (right-aligned for bids, left-aligned for asks). When one side has fewer levels than the other, the remaining rows are blank.

The Order Book tab populates from BookUpdated events. If no events have arrived yet, the display shows a placeholder message.

Data is preserved in a HashMap<String, OrderBookData> keyed by instrument ID, so switching instruments does not lose state.

Lag indicator: If the TUI falls behind the broadcast channel, the event log shows a ⚠ DROPPED N events entry. This happens when the terminal render loop (50 ms poll interval) cannot keep up with the event rate.

Tab 4 — Clock

Displays live clock and session information.

┌─ NadaTrade v0.1.4 ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Instruments │ Order Book │ Events │ Clock │ Ledger │ Admin                  │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                                                              │
│  Status:        ● RUNNING                                                    │
│  Clock Speed:   1x                                                           │
│  Sim Time:      14:32:45                                                     │
│  Session:       Trading                                                      │
│                                                                              │
│  Controls: Pause/Resume | Set Speed | Jump Time                              │
│                                                                              │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
FieldSource
StatusRUNNING (green) or PAUSED (red) from app.sim_info.paused
Clock SpeedMultiplier from ClockSpeedChanged events
Sim TimeExtracted from the sim_time field of the most recent event — displayed as HH:MM:SS
SessionCurrent session state as a string

Note on sim_time extraction: The TUI updates sim_info.last_sim_time from the sim_time field present on every ExchangeEvent variant. It reads the timestamp from whichever event arrived most recently, so the clock display reflects the event stream’s view of time — not wall-clock time.

The sim_time is formatted as %H:%M:%S (hours:minutes:seconds). Sub-second precision is not displayed in the TUI.

Tab 5 — Ledger

A participant-position matrix showing trade activity and P&L.

┌─ NadaTrade v0.1.4 ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Instruments │ Order Book │ Events │ Clock │ Ledger │ Admin                  │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                                                              │
│  Participant   Instrument  Qty        Avg Cost  Real P&L  Unreal P&L  Cash   │
│  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
│  BOT001        PETR4           100      38.50     0.0000     0.0000   -3850  │
│  BOT002        PETR4          -100      38.50     0.0000     0.0000    3850  │
│  BOT003        VALE3           500      61.20   150.0000    25.0000  -30600  │
│                                                                              │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
ColumnDescription
ParticipantParticipant ID (e.g. B for buyer, S for seller)
InstrumentInstrument ID
QtyNet position — positive for buys, negative for sells
Avg CostAverage cost basis (from LedgerRow.avg_cost)
Real P&LRealized profit/loss
Unreal P&LUnrealized (mark-to-market) P&L
CashCash balance (per currency)

The ledger is populated from TradePrinted events: the buyer gets a positive quantity entry and the seller gets a negative quantity entry. Characters are counted for the LedgerRow on both sides of each trade.

Rows are sorted alphabetically by participant ID. If no trades have occurred, the display shows a placeholder message.

Health Strip

The top bar spans the full terminal width and shows:

NadaTrade v0.1.4

Bordered block rendered in cyan. This strip is static and always visible, regardless of which tab is selected.

Architecture Notes

Event loop: The TUI runs a while app.running loop that:

  1. Draws the current frame via render()
  2. Polls for keyboard input with a 50 ms timeout (event::poll)
  3. Attempts a non-blocking read from the broadcast channel (try_recv)
  4. On Lagged(n), emits a ⚠ DROPPED N events log entry
  5. On Closed, logs a final message and the loop exits naturally on the next q press

Data structures: The App struct keeps all state in memory:

  • instruments: HashMap<String, InstrumentRow> — keyed by symbol
  • order_books: HashMap<String, OrderBookData> — keyed by symbol, contains bids and asks Vec<(Price, Qty)>
  • event_log: Vec<String> — capped at 200 entries, drained from the front
  • sim_info: SimInfo — clock speed, paused flag, last sim_time, session state
  • ledger_data: HashMap<String, LedgerRow> — keyed by participant ID

All state is reconstructed entirely from the event stream. There is no persistent on-disk state for the TUI — restarting it re-subscribes to the broadcast and re-populates from incoming events.

Thread model: The TUI runs on a std::thread (not a tokio task) because crossterm raw-mode terminal handling requires blocking synchronous I/O on the main thread. It owns the terminal handle, enters alternate screen mode on start, and restores the terminal on exit.

Sim time propagation: Every ExchangeEvent variant carries a sim_time: DateTime<Utc> field. The TUI extracts this via event_sim_time() and updates the clock display regardless of which event variant arrived. This means the displayed time advances as the event stream flows — it does not tick independently between events.

Troubleshooting

TUI not appearing: Verify [tui] enabled = true in config. If running standalone via cargo run -p nadatrade-admin, ensure the main binary is running and publishing events to the broadcast channel.

Blank tabs: Tabs populate lazily from events. If no events have been emitted (e.g., no orders, no session transitions), tabs will show placeholder text. Place an order to verify the pipeline.

Dropped events: The ⚠ DROPPED N events message means the terminal render loop is too slow for the event rate. This is normal during high-frequency bursts (e.g., replay mode at high speed). The TUI intentionally trades completeness for responsiveness.

Terminal not restored on exit: The TUI registers disable_raw_mode() and LeaveAlternateScreen in both the success and error paths. If the process is killed with SIGKILL (not SIGTERM/CTRL-C), the terminal may remain in raw mode. Run reset or stty sane to recover.

The Admin tab provides form-based management of instruments and participants.

KeyTypeDefaultDescription

When enabled = false (default), the TUI is not spawned and the main binary runs headless. You can still launch the TUI separately via cargo run -p nadatrade-admin, but it must connect to a running nadatrade-bin process that shares the broadcast channel — which, in practice, means running it in-process.

  • Admin API — REST API for managing instruments and participants
  • Observability & Metrics — Prometheus monitoring alongside the TUI

Admin API

The Admin API provides REST endpoints for managing instruments and participants. All endpoints require a JWT with "role": "admin".

Authentication

Generate an admin token using the same secret configured in ws_jwt_secret:

import jwt, time

secret = "your-jwt-secret"
token = jwt.encode(
    {"sub": "admin", "role": "admin", "exp": int(time.time()) + 3600},
    secret,
    algorithm="HS256"
)

Pass the token in the Authorization header:

curl -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" http://localhost:9824/v1/admin/instruments

Or as a query parameter:

curl "http://localhost:9824/v1/admin/instruments?token=$TOKEN"

Non-admin tokens (without "role": "admin") receive HTTP 403.

Endpoints

Instruments

List all instruments

GET /v1/admin/instruments

Response: [ { instrument fields }, ... ]

Get instrument by name

GET /v1/admin/instruments/{name}

Response: { instrument fields } or 404.

Create instrument

POST /v1/admin/instruments
Content-Type: application/json

Request body (all Decimal fields are strings):

{
  "name": "BTCBRL",
  "currency": "BRL",
  "tick_size": "0.01",
  "lot_size": "0.001",
  "min_qty_d": "0.001",
  "max_qty_d": "100",
  "price_band": "0.05",
  "md_depth_max": 20,
  "session_type": "continuous_24x7",
  "timezone": "America/Sao_Paulo",
  "trading_days": "Mon,Tue,Wed,Thu,Fri,Sat,Sun",
  "settlement_cycle": "T0",
  "settlement_currency": "BRL"
}

Response: 201 with created instrument. Required fields: name, currency, tick_size, lot_size, session_type, settlement_currency. All others use defaults.

Update instrument

PUT /v1/admin/instruments/{name}
Content-Type: application/json

Same body as create. The name field in the body is ignored (uses path parameter). Response: 200 with updated instrument. 404 if not found.

Delete instrument

DELETE /v1/admin/instruments/{name}

Response: 204 No Content. 404 if not found.

Participants

List all participants

GET /v1/admin/participants

Get participant

GET /v1/admin/participants/{comp_id}

Create participant

POST /v1/admin/participants

Request body example:

{
  "comp_id": "BOT003",
  "display_name": "Arbitrage Gamma",
  "active": true,
  "initial_cash": { "BRL": "500000.00" },
  "latency_profile": "co_location",
  "ws_secret": "bot003-secret",
  "position_limits": { "PETR4": "50000" },
  "md_extra_delay_us": 1000
}

Response: 201.

Update participant

PUT /v1/admin/participants/{comp_id}

Delete participant

DELETE /v1/admin/participants/{comp_id}

Force Reload

POST /v1/admin/reload

Re-reads all instruments and participants from the database and rebuilds the in-memory registries. Useful after direct database modifications.

Response:

{ "reloaded": true, "instruments": 5, "participants": 3 }

Bootstrap

On first startup, if the instruments table is empty, NadaTrade automatically seeds it from the TOML files in the instruments/ directory. Same for participants/. After the initial seed, the database is the source of truth and TOML files are ignored.

How Bootstrap Works

  1. At startup, the binary checks if database tables have any rows
  2. If instruments table is empty: reads all .toml files from the configured paths.instruments directory, inserts each instrument into the database
  3. If participants table is empty: reads all .toml files from paths.participants, inserts each participant
  4. The in-memory registries are then populated from the database
  5. On subsequent startups, the database is the authoritative source — TOML files are not re-read

This means:

  • First run: define your instruments in TOML files, they get imported
  • After that: use the Admin API to add/update/delete instruments and participants — TOML changes are ignored
  • To re-import from TOML: drop and recreate the database, or use the POST /v1/admin/reload endpoint to force a reload

Hot Reload via TOML

The notify file watcher monitors the instruments/ and participants/ directories for changes. When a TOML file is added, modified, or deleted, the respective registry broadcasts a RegistryChange event:

  • Added — new instrument/participant available for trading
  • Updated — existing config fields changed (e.g. tick_size, session times)
  • Delisted — instrument removed from trading; open orders cancelled with InstrumentDelisted

Changes are applied on the next ClockTick — they do not take effect mid-matching-cycle.

TOML vs Admin API

MethodBest forPersistence
TOML filesInitial setup, GitOps, config-as-codeOne-time import on first run
Admin APIRuntime changes, automated provisioningDirectly to database
POST /v1/admin/reloadForce re-import from TOMLOverwrites database entries

Error Responses

CodeMeaning
201Created successfully
204Deleted successfully
400Invalid request body (JSON parse error or missing required field)
401Missing or invalid JWT token
403Valid token but missing "role": "admin" claim
404Instrument or participant not found
500Database error or registry sync failure

Python Examples

Add a new instrument

import requests

token = "your-admin-jwt"
headers = {"Authorization": f"Bearer {token}"}

resp = requests.post("http://localhost:9824/v1/admin/instruments", json={
    "name": "NEW-INSTRUMENT",
    "currency": "BRL",
    "tick_size": "0.01",
    "lot_size": "100",
    "session_type": "session_based",
    "settlement_currency": "BRL"
}, headers=headers)

print(resp.status_code, resp.json())

Add a new participant (bot)

resp = requests.post("http://localhost:9824/v1/admin/participants", json={
    "comp_id": "NEW-BOT",
    "display_name": "My New Strategy",
    "active": True,
    "initial_cash": {"BRL": "1000000.00"},
    "latency_profile": "co_location",
    "ws_secret": "my-bot-secret"
}, headers={"Authorization": f"Bearer {token}"})

print(resp.status_code)

Deployment

This guide covers deploying NadaTrade on a server for multiple remote users — on your LAN, VPS, or cloud instance. All participants connect to this single host.

Choose Your Method

MethodBest forComplexity
Docker ComposeVPS, cloud instances, quick setupLow
Bare-metal + systemdDedicated servers, low-latencyMedium
Binary downloadDevelopment, CI pipelinesLow

Docker Compose (Recommended)

The repo includes docker-compose.yml for a production-ready stack with PostgreSQL 16 and the nadatrade binary.

git clone https://github.com/nadawrks/nadatrade
cd nadatrade
cp .env.example .env

Configure Secrets

Edit .env with strong values. At minimum:

NADATRADE_JWT_SECRET=$(openssl rand -hex 32)
PG_PASSWORD=$(openssl rand -hex 16)

The .env file is in .gitignore — never commit it.

Start the Stack

docker compose up -d

This starts two services:

  • postgres — PostgreSQL 16, data persisted in the pgdata named volume
  • nadatrade — the exchange binary, maps ports 9823 (FIX) and 9824 (WS/HTTP)

Verify

curl http://<server-ip>:9824/healthz
# {"status":"ok"}

Volume Management

Database data survives container restarts via the pgdata volume. To reset everything:

docker compose down -v   # removes volume — data is gone
docker compose up -d     # fresh start

Pull Pre-Built Image

Instead of building locally:

docker pull ghcr.io/nadawrks/nadatrade:latest

Then set NADATRADE_IMAGE=ghcr.io/nadawrks/nadatrade:latest in .env before docker compose up.

Bare-Metal with systemd

For dedicated servers where you want the binary running directly on the host (for lowest latency or custom kernel tuning).

Install

sudo ./deploy/install.sh

This script:

  1. Creates a nadatrade system user
  2. Downloads the latest binary to /usr/local/bin/nadatrade
  3. Generates a random JWT secret
  4. Installs a systemd service at /etc/systemd/system/nadatrade.service

Manage the Service

sudo systemctl start nadatrade
sudo systemctl status nadatrade
sudo systemctl enable nadatrade   # start on boot

View logs:

journalctl -u nadatrade -f

Configuration

The systemd unit reads environment from /etc/nadatrade/env. Add your secrets there:

# /etc/nadatrade/env
DATABASE_URL=postgres://nadatrade:secret@localhost:5432/nadatrade
NADATRADE_JWT_SECRET=<hex-secret>
SIM_MODE=real_time

Binary Download

Download a pre-built binary from the GitHub Releases page:

curl -fsSL https://github.com/nadawrks/nadatrade/releases/latest/download/nadatrade \
  -o /usr/local/bin/nadatrade
chmod +x /usr/local/bin/nadatrade

export DATABASE_URL="postgres://nadatrade@localhost/nadatrade"
export JWT_SECRET="$(openssl rand -hex 32)"
export SIM_MODE="real_time"

nadatrade --config config/nadatrade.toml

Network Configuration

Ports

PortProtocolPurpose
9823TCPFIX 4.2 gateway
9824TCPWebSocket + HTTP (/healthz, /metrics, /v1/admin)

Both ports bind to 0.0.0.0 by default. For a multi-tenant server, you want these reachable by your bot developers. Restrict in config if needed:

[server]
fix_listen_addr = "10.0.0.5:9823"    # internal NIC only
ws_listen_addr = "10.0.0.5:9824"

Firewall

Allow only the ports you need. Example with ufw:

ufw allow 9823/tcp   # FIX
ufw allow 9824/tcp   # WS + HTTP

Do not expose PostgreSQL (5432) to the internet.

TLS Termination

NadaTrade FIX and WebSocket protocols do not include native TLS. Use a reverse proxy or tunnel for encrypted transport.

Option 1: Reverse Proxy (Recommended)

Use nginx or Caddy to terminate TLS for the WebSocket/HTTP port (9824).

nginx example (/etc/nginx/sites-available/nadatrade):

server {
    listen 443 ssl;
    server_name exchange.example.com;

    ssl_certificate     /etc/ssl/certs/exchange.pem;
    ssl_certificate_key /etc/ssl/private/exchange.key;

    location / {
        proxy_pass http://127.0.0.1:9824;
        proxy_http_version 1.1;
        proxy_set_header Upgrade $http_upgrade;
        proxy_set_header Connection "upgrade";
        proxy_set_header Host $host;
        proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-For $proxy_add_x_forwarded_for;
    }
}

Clients then connect to wss://exchange.example.com/v1/orders?token=....

Option 2: stunnel for FIX

FIX clients connect via raw TCP. To encrypt FIX traffic, wrap it with stunnel on both ends:

Server stunnel config (/etc/stunnel/nadatrade-fix.conf):

[fix-tls]
accept  = 0.0.0.0:19823
connect = 127.0.0.1:9823
cert    = /etc/ssl/certs/exchange.pem
key     = /etc/ssl/private/exchange.key

Clients connect to port 19823 (TLS) and stunnel decrypts to 9823 (plain). The FIX client sees a plain TCP stream.

Option 3: VPN / WireGuard

For internal/team use, run nadatrade on a private network (VPC, VPN, WireGuard) and have bot developers connect via VPN. No TLS needed at the application layer.

Multi-Tenant Setup

Participant Registration

Each bot developer needs a participant entry. Add them to participants/participants.toml:

[participants.alice]
comp_id = "ALICE"
display_name = "Alice's Trading Bot"
active = true
initial_cash = { BRL = "1000000.00" }

[participants.bob]
comp_id = "BOB"
display_name = "Bob's Market Maker"
active = true
initial_cash = { BRL = "500000.00" }

Or use the Admin API to add participants at runtime:

curl -X POST http://<host>:9824/v1/admin/participants \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $ADMIN_TOKEN" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"comp_id":"ALICE","display_name":"Alice","active":true,"initial_cash":{"BRL":"1000000.00"}}'

Distribute Credentials

For each participant, generate a JWT token using your ws_jwt_secret:

import jwt, time
token = jwt.encode(
    {"sub": "ALICE", "exp": int(time.time()) + 31536000},  # 1 year
    secret, algorithm="HS256"
)

Give each developer:

  • Server hostname/IP and port
  • Their JWT token (or the secret to generate their own)
  • FIX credentials if using FIX (username + password from participant config)

Isolation

  • Order fills are private — Alice cannot see Bob’s fills or order book activity
  • Trade prints are public — everyone sees market-wide trades
  • Admin API requires a separate admin JWT token with "role": "admin"
  • Participants operate within their own cash and position limits

Backup & Restore

Database Backup

pg_dump -U nadatrade -h localhost nadatrade > backup.sql

The events table grows continuously. For large deployments, use monthly partition-based backup to skip old partitions:

pg_dump -U nadatrade -h localhost nadatrade \
  --table=events_y202606 --table=events_y202607 \
  --table=snapshots --table=persist_meta \
  > partial_backup.sql

Restore

psql -U nadatrade -h localhost nadatrade < backup.sql

NadaTrade replays events from the last snapshot on restart — no additional restore step needed.

Resource Sizing

WorkloadRAMCPUDisk (events table)
1-5 instruments, 10 bots512 MB1 core~100 MB/day
10-50 instruments, 50 bots2 GB2 cores~1 GB/day
100+ instruments, 100+ bots8 GB4 cores~10 GB/day

These are rough estimates. Actual usage depends on order rate and persistence batch settings.

Connection Limits

The FIX acceptor spawns one tokio task per connection. There is no built-in connection limit — use OS-level limits (ulimit -n) or a connection-counting gauge in the metrics.

Production Checklist

  • Change ws_jwt_secret from changeme
  • Set strong PG_PASSWORD
  • Enable TLS (reverse proxy or stunnel)
  • Restrict fix_listen_addr / ws_listen_addr if not all interfaces needed
  • Configure firewall to only expose ports 9823 and 9824
  • Set up Prometheus scraping of /metrics
  • Set up log aggregation (journald or docker logs)
  • Tune persist.snapshot_every_events for your recovery SLA
  • Configure database backups (at minimum, pg_dump cron)
  • Test recovery from backup before going live

Observability & Metrics

NadaTrade exports 24 Prometheus metrics and provides HTTP endpoints for health checks and metrics scraping. This page documents everything you can monitor.

Endpoints

EndpointProtocolPortPurpose
GET /healthzHTTP9824Liveness check
GET /metricsHTTP9824Prometheus text format metrics

Both endpoints are served on the same port as the WebSocket gateway (default 9824). No authentication is required for /healthz or /metrics.

Health Check

curl http://<host>:9824/healthz
# {"status":"ok"}

Returns HTTP 200 with {"status":"ok"} when the server is accepting connections and the event loop is running. Use this for Docker healthchecks, Kubernetes liveness probes, or load balancer health checks.

Docker Compose healthcheck:

healthcheck:
  test: ["CMD", "curl", "-f", "http://localhost:9824/healthz"]
  interval: 10s
  timeout: 5s
  retries: 3

Scraping Setup

Add to your prometheus.yml:

scrape_configs:

    scrape_interval: 15s
    static_configs:
      - targets: ['<host>:9824']
